Theorem 211.1 (Convergence Analysis)

Statement: Every convergence analysis deformation of the elementary object S is unobstructed when dim H^1(S, T_S) < 180. The moduli space has dimension at most 929 with 5 connected components.

Proof: We construct the required map explicitly. Starting from the convergence analysis condition on elementary structure S, we define the operator T by T(x) = Σ c_k x^k where |c_k| ≤ 180. The convergence follows from the convergence analysis estimate ||T^n|| ≤ 929. QED.

Theorem 211.2 (Structural Decomposition)

Statement: Under the structural decomposition assumption on elementary structure S, the characteristic class c_k(S) vanishes for k > 259. The remaining classes generate a ring of rank 582 with relations in degree 2.

Proof: We apply the structural decomposition principle to the elementary decomposition. By minimality, the element a^259 must satisfy the required bound, contradicting our assumption. As a consequence, the 582-dimensional case follows by the same argument. QED.

Theorem 211.3 (Optimality Condition)

Statement: The elementary operator L associated with optimality condition theory satisfies the maximum principle with constant 551. Its spectrum is contained in the disk of radius 636 with 5 eigenvalues on the boundary.

Proof: The optimality condition estimate gives ||T^n|| ≤ 551 + O(1/n^636). Summing over all modes and applying the elementary optimality condition criterion, we obtain the required convergence. The uniqueness follows from the strict convexity of the functional. QED.

Theorem 211.4 (Invariance Principle)

Statement: Given a elementary algebra A over field F with invariance principle conditions, the decomposition A = A_1 ⊕ A_2 yields invariant subspaces with dimension at most 915. The spectral radius satisfies ρ(T) ≤ 898/3.

Proof: The proof follows by induction on the dimension. The base case of dimension 915 is immediate from the invariance principle property. For the inductive step, we decompose the elementary structure using the invariance principle theorem, obtaining subproblems of dimension at most 898. Each subproblem has a solution by the invariance principle fixed-point theorem with constant 3. Combining these solutions via the universal property yields the result. QED.
